666. THOMAS CONDON was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, three pair of stays, value 7 s. the goods of Alfred Carter.
NATHANIEL ELLIS. I am collector of the Chelsea Water-works. On the 19th of March, about seven o'clock at night, I was in Oxford-street, and saw five or six lads near Carter's shop. I suspected them to be pick-pockets, and felt to see if my pocket was secure - two of them fell back, the prisoner was one of them. I turned round to see if they were near me, and saw the prisoner coming out of the shop, tucking something under his jacket. I said
"You have been stealing something" - he said,
"Me, Sir;" I said
"Yes; what have you here," he threw the stays down - I secured him, part of his cuff gave way and I collared him.
MARY ANN COOPER. The stays are the property of my employer, Alfred Carter.
GUILTY. Aged 16.

667. JOHN TIMMINS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, three trowels, value 3 s. the goods of Joseph Brown.
JOSEPH BROWN. I am a plaisterer. On the 7th of April, I was working in Regency-street, and left my trowels in the house while I went to dinner - on returning I missed them and next morning found it in the officer's possession.
RICHARD ROWE WICKS. I am a constable. On the 7th of March, about one o'clock, I saw the prisoner with a quantity of bolts under his jacket, and watched him into an old iron shop, in Short's Gardens, went in and found him offering them for sale. I took him in custody.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. They swear false, they were my old masters.

668. JOSEPH PEELING and JOHN BILLETT were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of March, one bullock's tongue 5 s., the goods of Isaac Page.
DANIEL COLE. I am shopman to Mr. Isaac Page, an oilman, who lives in Great Portland-street; this tongue was stolen from the door.
CHARLES READ. I am an officer. About eight o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoners, suspecting them, I followed them, and just as I turned the corner, one of them snatched this tongue down - I saw them run from the shop with it - they had nothing before. I followed and stopped Billet, he said he had it of Peeling, and Peeling said he did not have it from him.
JOHN DAVIS. I met Read, following the prisoners. I went on; soon after the prisoners came by me with something - they had nothing before; we stopped them, one said it was a tongue which he had from the other, and the other said he knew nothing about it.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
PEELING'S Defence. I met this man, he said he was going my way; he left me, and soon after brought the tongue up, saying he found it.
BILLETT'S Defence. They have both told an untruth - the tongue laid inside some rails in the New-road - I took it out.
PEELING - GUILTY. Aged 19.
BILLETT - GUILTY. Aged 23.
Transported for Seven Years,
Second Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Recorder.

APPLEBY'S Defence. I heard the cry, and witness stopt me.

673. CHARLES BARBER was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of March, one carpet, value 30 s., the goods of James Miller.
WILLIAM WADE. On the 15th of March, about seven in the evening, I was near Clerkenwell-green, and could see Miller's the broker's shop; I saw the prisoner on the Green, and saw him take the carpet, which hung on the pailings outside the shop, and run away with it; he had two more with him; he ran down the Green, and I showed him to Miller's young man. I saw him a few minutes after, and knew him to be the boy who received it. Another boy took it and threw it down; the prisoner took a bag and put over it, and ran down the Green with it.
SAMUEL BECKWITH. I was on Clerkenwell-green, and saw the prisoner run down the Green with the carpet under his arm; I watched and followed him about three hundred yards, and saw him put it into a house, he just threw it inside the door, and walked away and joined another boy at the corner of the street, who was waiting for him; they walked away up the Green: several of them said to him,
"Are you in it?" I found Miller had lost a carpet: Miller and I went to search the house; the carpet was gone, and has never been found. When we returned, the prisoner was taken: I am sure he is the boy.
JAMES MILLER. I am owner of the shop. I lost a carpet on this evening, it hung on the rails in front of my house. I have not seen it since. The prisoner was taken about an hour and a half after, and denied the charge.
JOHN WIGGINS. I am an officer. I was sent for, and secured the prisoner, and left him at the house while I went to look for the carpet, but could not find it. I asked how he came to steal it? he said he knew nothing about it. I asked his name? he said he did not know his name.
Prisoner's Defence. Two lads tapped me on the shoulder and said,
"Has your father lost a carpet;" I went with them directly and said I knew nothing about it.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Three Months, and Whipped.
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.
[trialAccount t18210411-190]
674. CAROLINE HUMPHREYS and MARY JONES, were indicted for stealing on the 12th of March, one counterpane, value 7 s., the goods of Frances Avis, widow.
FRANCIS AVIS. I am a widow, and live in Tower-street, Seven Dials, and take in washing. About 10 o'clock in the morning, I hung this counterpane in the yard of my house to dry: it is a private yard. I missed it in about half an hour, and have not seen it since. It was a large white knotted one. I know nothing of the prisoners. I do not wash for either of them.
ELIZA COCHRANE. On the 12th of March, about half past 10 o'clock I was sitting at Mr. Spicer's door, which is three doors from Avis's. I saw the prisoners come out of Spicer's wine vaults: Humphries clapped her hand in her bosom and said,
"D - n my eyes if I have not lost it." She took her shawl and covered her bosom. I saw them both turn into Avis's; in about five minutes, Humphries came out with a large white counterpane - the corner hung down at her feet - she carried it openly. Jones was with her. They went went down through Lombard-court. Avis came out in about half an hour, and I told her which way they went. I knew them both before, they are loose women, and live in Seven Dials.
JURY. Q. Why not tell the prosecutrix? A. She takes in washing. They brought it out openly - I did not know any thing was wrong. I sell fish in the streets.
WILLIAM LEE. I am a patrol of Bow-street. I apprehended the prisoners about 1 o'clock - Humphries was much intoxicated.
JOHN MOKELAND. I am a fishmonger, and live at No. 8, Lombard-court. On the 12th of March, about 11 o'clock, I saw Humphries (who I knew before), come by my shop with the other prisoner; Humphries had a great white bundle under her arm - I think it was a counterpane.
HUMPHRIES' Defence. I never saw that woman. She owes me a spite, because I had a few words with her.
JONES'S Defence. I never saw any thing in her possession.
COCHRANE. I never spoke to her.
HUMPHRIES - GUILTY. Aged 26.
JONES - GUILTY. Aged 34.
Confined Three Months.
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.
[trialAccount t18210411-191]
675. EMANUEL PROCTER & CHARLES PROCTER were indicted for feloniously receiving on the 5th of December, 2 seals, value 3 l. 17 s.; and 1 watch-key, value 10 s., the goods of John George Fearn, of which Samuel Procter was at the present Session convicted of stealing, they knowing them to be stolen.
MR. JOHN GEORGE FEARN. I am a jeweller, and live in Cornhill; I lost a variety of seals. Samuel Procter was my porter, and younger brother to the prisoners. I prosecuted him this Session, and he was convicted. I have seen both the prisoners at my house two or three times visiting their brother.
DAVID SIMPSON. I am shopman to Mr. Fearn; there were a quantity of seals missing. I went about to the pawnbrokers with the officers and found several seals out, but wasnot present when these were found, they were brought to me and I identified them.
WILLIAM ALDRIDGE. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Orange-street, Bloomsbury. On the 5th of December, 2 seals and a gold key were pawned with me by the two prisoners, openly in their own name, for 2 l. I produced them, they are the same, and the duplicate tallies with them.
THOMAS MAYHEW. I am an officer. I detained the prisoners as Giltspur-street compter, where they came to see their brother, who was in custody. When the brother was committed, what he said was not taken down, no inducement was held out to him. He said he gave them the seals to pawn, and told them a man had given them to him.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
E. PROCTER'S Defence. My brother stated before the Jury that he gave them to us, saying they had been made a present to him by some workmen, and desired us to get 2 l. on them. We have known Aldridge for years. I am a jeweller, and have pawned articles with him before.
C. PROCTER'S Defence. He said a workman gave them to him, and being in the same business, I have had presents made to me, and had not the least reason to suspect him.
NOT GUILTY.
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.
[trialAccount t18210411-192]
676. ESAU HART was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, one shilling, the property of Robert Crane.
ROBERT CRANE. I am a milkman, and live at Hoxton. The prisoner is a stranger. I missed money off my mantle shelf, in the front room down stairs, several times, and so I marked a shilling and sixpence, and put them on the mantle shelf, and left Edwards to watch, and afterwards found the prisoner in custody. It was on the 23rd of March, between six and seven o'clock in the morning.
THOMAS EDWARDS. I am a working silversmith. Crane set me to watch in the back premises, and I saw the prisoner come in under pretence to warm his hands, take the shilling off the mantle shelf and put it in his pocket. I never saw him before; he is a neighbour's child, but I did not know it. His father bears a good character.
RICHARD CONSTANTINE. I am a constable. The boy was brought to me. I searched him, and found the shilling in his left side pocket. He said he was going to the baker's for his father, and lost a shilling, and took this to pay for the bread, that his father might not find it out.
ROBERT CRANE. The shilling is mine.
Prisoner's Defence. I throw myself on the mercy of the Court.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Whipped and discharged.
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.

677. JOSHUA JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, one blanket, value 3 s., the goods of George Howard Denham.
HANNAH FOSTER DENHAM. I am the wife of George Howard Denham, a publican. We live in Prince's-street, Westminster. The prisoner was quartered on us. We have missed things for the last six months, while he was with us. I did not miss this blanket, but on Saturday the 24th of February Donahoo brought it to me. I know it to be ours; it was on a man's bed in the same room where the prisoner slept.
MICHAEL DONAHOO. I am servant to Mr. Denham. On the 24th of February, between six and seven o'clock in the morning I met the prisoner in Tothill-street with this blanket, and followed him down to New Tothill-street, then stopped him, and said this is my master's property. He told me to go home, and he would give me 1 s. to say nothing about it. He knew me, I took the blanket home.
GEORGE POPLE, I am a constable. The prosecutor applied to me to apprehend the prisoner. He belongs to the 1st regiment of guards. I took him in custody that evening, he would say nothing to me, he would not tell me his name.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw the boy or blanket.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Transported for Seven Years.
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.

678. THOMAS JONES and JAMES LACEY, were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of April, one mattress, value 5 s.; one bed, value 2 l.; one bolster, value 10 s.; two pillow cases, value 5 s.; two pillows, value 10 s.; and two blankets, value 10 s., the goods of John Egleton.
JAMES MITCHELL. I had the care of a house, No. 4, Dalby-terrace, City-road, which belonged to Mr. John Egleton, who has now removed to Liverpool; he moved a little before Quarter day, and left me in care of his house and furniture. On the 13th of April I left the house locked up safe, at a little after seven o'clock in the evening, and about nine o'clock was informed of the robbery. I went to the house, and found the gates and doors open, and these articles missing. I found them that night at the watch-house, with the prisoners in custody.
JOHN KILLDRUP. I am servant at the City of London public house, City-road. On the 13th of April, about ten minutes before nine o'clock at night, I was about six doors from this house, and saw Lacey go into the garden and go down the area. I had seen the prisoners before, walking up and down, in company with another man and woman. I then saw Jones go in, and in a short time saw him come out with a bundle, and directly after Lacey came out with another bundle. I saw Mr. Cheslin, my master, coming, and I told him. Brown took Jones alongside his bundle. Lacey threw down his bundle and ran away. I and Hunter pursued him to the corner of River terrace, and took him without losing sight of him. His bundle remained where he threw it. Both bundles were taken to the watch-house.
JOHN BROWN. I saw the last witness, he gave me information. I saw three men and a woman walking about, - I do not know them. I went with my beer, and as I came down the road I saw two bundles falling. I saw one bundle drop, and Lacey ran from where it dropped. He was taken in my sight, - the bundles were taken to the watch-house. I did not see Jones till he was at the watch-house.
JAMES BROWN. I am a plumber and glazier, and live atPentonville. I received information from Cheslin, and went with him, and met Jones with a load on his back. I immediately collared and held him; - he dropped the bundle, and was taken to the watch-house. I do not know who picked the bundle up.
WILLIAM HUNTER. I received information, and went and met Lacey and collared him. Brown took Jones. I asked Lacey what he had there, he said a bed, and threw it on my arm, and broke from me. I pursued, and took him at the corner of River-terrace. Jordan picked the bundle up.
JAMES JORDAN. I received information and went to Egleton's house, and saw Lacey with the bed on his back; he was secured. I saw them both coming down the road, with each a bundle, - they were both secured. I took Lacey's bundle to the watch-house, and Cheslin's servant carried the other.
ROBERT SMITH. I am an officer. I was on duty in the City-road, - heard the cry of stop thief, - ran, and found Lacey in custody. I took him to Cheslin's, and there found Jones in custody. I took both to the watch-house, and sent the bundles there. I went to the house, and found a great deal of the property moved, ready to be carried away, a looking glass and fire irons were moved. I found a hammer and two screw drivers there.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
JONES, - GUILTY. Aged 28.
LACEY, - GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.

680. SAMUEL STEVENS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, three spoons, value 10 s., the goods of John George Warigner.
ELIZA CLARK. I am servant to Dr. J. G. Warigner, who lives at Kingsland. On the 3d of April a boy (not the prisoner) came about some potatoes; we ordered some - then the prisoner came with the boy; we had not seen him before - they carried the sack of potatoes down stairs together; my mistress had been to the door to look at them. The boys waited down stairs for me to take them the money; some silver spoons were on the kitchen shelf over the dresser; they took the potatoes into the back kitchen. I was cleaning knives while they waited for the money; they were in the kitchen about ten minutes - mistress paid them, and they went away about eleven o'clock. About an hour after they were gone, I missed two salt and a dessert spoon. I saw them at Worship-street last Monday fortnight.
MRS. MARIA WARIGNER. Another boy came first, then the prisoner came with him, to take the potatoes down into the back kitchen; they remained there about six minutes while they emptied them. I left them in the passage between the two kitchens. I was up stairs a few minutes; they said they would not have the money till I had tried the potatoes to see if they were good; but when they emptied them, they wanted their money directly: I paid them 18 d. short. After they were gone I missed the spoons, and found them afterwards at the office. The boy asked for the money, the prisoner did not speak; he heard what was said.
WILLIAM ATTFIELD. I am a constable. I took the prisoner into custody on the day mentioned in the indictment. was coming from Walthamstow, and received information of some men who were selling potatoes about Hackney being suspected of a robbery. I met the two; I took the other, and left him in care of a gardener while I run after the prisoner. I caught him, and found the spoons wrapped in paper in his pocket.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I picked them up with three potatoes.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Six Months and Whipped.
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.
[trialAccount t18210411-197]
681. MICHAEL MACKIN and JOSEPH DOGHERTY were indicted for stealing, on the 23d of March, one bed, value 20 s.; two blankets, value 7 s.; one bolster, value 5 s.; and one saucepan lid, value 3 s., the goods of William Charles Fortescue, Viscount Claremont, of Ireland.
STAIR STUART. I have the care of Lord Claremont's house in Berkeley-square; his names are William Charles Fortescue. On the 23d of March I had a message from his lordship, who lodged in Duke-street, St. James's, desiring me to deliver a hamper to the prisoner Dogherty, whom his lordship permitted to live in the house in Berkeley-square out of humanity. These things were taken from the house in Berkeley-square. My wife gave me information, and I missed a bed, a bolster, and two blankets: they were taken while I went with a message to his lordship. I had left Dogherty and my wife in the house. The things were in the room where he slept. I had seen them some time before; he was out when I missed them. He returned in about an hour. I asked him what he had done with the bed and blankets? he said he had sold them; fell on his knees, and begged I would not inform his lordship of the transaction. He was in liquor; it was between one and two o'clock in the day time. He told us where he sold them. I found them at Marlborough-street. I suspected Mackin, as I had seen him three or four times at the house; he came to see Dogherty. Somebody gave me information. I went and found another bed had been taken out backwards; he owned to taking that, fell on his knees again, and begged for mercy.
MARY APPLETON. I live at No. 5, David-street, Berkeley-square, and keep a chandler's shop. The day before Dogherty was apprehended, he came to me and asked me if I bought old feathers. I bought a bed and bolster of himthat day for 15 s., it was a small bed. I gave that to the officer. Next day I bought a tin can of him. I bought two blankets of the other prisoner before the 22nd, at two different times. I sold one, and gave the other to the officer. I bought that about the 18th.
CHARLOTTE TUDOR. I was hired in January to clean the coppers at his lordship's, they all had their lids on them.
SAMUEL PLANK. I am an officer. On the 23d of March the prisoners were brought to me charged with this robbery. Dogherty was imploring his lordship's mercy. I went and searched Appleton's house, and found the bed, bolster, and blanket, and a stewpan lid, with the figures 3-2 L C on it. She said, in the prisoners' presence, that she had the stew-pan lid from Mackin; he said he received it from Dogherty, who did not deny it.
ANDREW RIGGINS. I am a plaisterer, and live at No. 14, Great Mary-le-bone-lane. On the 23d of March, in the morning, I was at work in his lordship's house; Mackin came in with me as I went to work. I did not see him again till breakfast time, when he came along the passage to me and said,
"Is the old gentleman up?" meaning Stuart I said,
"I dont think he is." I saw him go under the stairs and take out something, what it was I did not know. I went to my work, and between four and five o'clock Stuart informed me somebody was forcing a bed through the two pair of stairs back window. I ran up and saw Dogherty standing there, and asked him about it. He said he had hid the bed. He took me up into a room where there was some hair, and under the hair was the bed. I suspected Mackin, and saw him about six o'clock, and took him. His lordship came soon after. I heard Mackin tell Dogherty he should not have taken the things away by day-time, but by night.
JOHN KING. I am beadle of St. George's parish, Hanover-square. When the prisoners were in my custody, I heard Dogherty say to Mackin,
"You know I told you to do what you did in the evening;" and Mackin said,
"I would not have had it happened for 50 l."
(Property produced and sworn to.)
MARY APPLETON re-examined. I had the copper lid from Mackin, he brought it on the 23d, and asked 3 s. 6 d. for it. I said I was no judge of such a thing. He said he would call for it in an hour. He called, and said he would give it to the person who gave it him to sell.
MACKIN - GUILTY. Aged 60.
DOGHERTY - GUILTY. Aged 32.
Confined Twelve Months.
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.
[trialAccount t18210411-198]

682. HENRY NEWLAND and BENJAMIN STEVENSON, were indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, certain fixtures (i. e.) forty-eight panes of glass, value 5 l. belonging to Mark Cohen, and fixed to a building of his.
SECOND COUNT, Not stating them to be fixtures.
STEVENSON pleaded Guilty. Aged 29.
MARK COHEN. I am a paper-stainer, and live in Devonshire-street, Commercial-road. On Sunday evening the 15th of April, about half past seven o'clock, the watchman came and enquired if I had moved any sashes from my new house; I went round with him to the house, and at the beginning of the street I looked up to the house, and saw the prisoner Newland in the act of taking a sash out of the first floor window of my house; on looking at the other window of the room, I saw Stevenson taking the sash out of that window - I saw them actually doing it; the watchman said,
"They are now in the house." He ran round to the back of the house - nobody lives in it - it is just finished: I unlocked the door; the watchman said I had better wait outside. I ran out and saw Stevenson attempt to jump out of the window; the watchman went up and took them both in custody in the room. I examined the back door, and found a bolt wrenched off: I had seen the windows safe in the morning. We found a chisel and a bag there.
JOHN CLOVER. I am the watchman. I assisted the last witness in taking the prisoners. I found Dornain had them in custody. I found six squares of glass in a bag they had been taken out of one of the sashes.
JOSEPH DORNAIN. I am a watchman. I assisted in taking the prisoners in the back room first floor of Cohen's empty house; they said nothing. I found a chisel in the room covered with putty.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
NEWLAND'S Defence. Stevenson came into the Red Lion and drank with me, and said he would give me 2 s. to carry his tools; I went with him, but touched nothing.
NEWLAND - GUILTY. Aged 43.
Both transported for Seven Years.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Arabin.

683. MARY M'CULLOFF was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of April, one coat, value 18 s., one jacket, value 18 s. and one handkerchief, value 1 s. the goods of William Connor.
WILLIAM CONNOR. I am a mariner. On the 12th of April I went to see a friend at Charing-cross, and stopped till 12 o'clock at night, and made my way as far as White Hart-street. I met a woman, who took me to Bradfield's house, White Hart-yard, Drury-lane, to sleep; I went to bed, and about 7 in the morning the prisoner came and awoke me: I had not seen her before. She demanded the bed and said it was hers, and she wanted to lay down. I said I was rather heavy, and had been drinking and wished to lay longer, and that I had paid for my lodging, but did not object to her laying down; she laid down in her clothes, and in about an hour and a half she said she was very dry, and asked if I had any money; I gave her 3 d. and she went away, and between 10 and 11 o'clock she came up; I saw her take my coat, handkerchief, and jacket, and ran away with them - I could not pursue her. The woman of the house came up. I found my jacket at the pawnbrokers.
JAMES FLETCHER. I am a constable. I was sent for, and took her about 3 o'clock on Friday afternoon, the 13th, I told her the charge - she denied it; she was very drunk. Itook her to the watch-house with a deal of trouble. Next morning I found the jacket and handkerchief at Mr. Turner's. I have known her two years, she lived with her mother in Little Wild-street. The pawnbroker is not here.
Court to CONNOR. Q. Are you married? A. No. I had no money except what paid for the lodging. I paid the woman for the lodging, and she went away. I was sober in the morning, and was obliged to stop there till night, when the landlord lent me clothes to go to Bow Street.
Prisoner's Defence. He came up to me and said, a woman who had been with him had stolen his money and clothes; I was tipsy, and broke some panes of glass, and was taken in custody for that.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
W. CONNOR. On my oath it is false, I never said another woman robbed me.
NOT GUILTY.
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.

684. WILLIAM SACK and GEORGE SMITH were indicted for stealing on the 16th of April, 100 lbs. of lead, value 18 s. belonging to James Barker, and fixed to a certain dwelling-house of his.
SECOND COUNT, stating it to be fixed to a building of his.
WILLIAM COURT. I am a sawyer, and live in Mr. Barker's house to take care of it; he does not reside there. On the 16th of April, about a quarter past nine, my wife awoke me and said she heard a noise in the house: I jumped up and threw up the window, and saw two men in the act of rolling a piece of lead off the water closet. I did not know either of them: the one on my right hand had a blue coat on, and the other had a light fustian jacket. I called out stop thief, they ran and I pursued them; they jumped over the foundation, and I saw Sack taken by a gentleman as he got over the wall; he was one of them. The other in the light jacket got over the other corner and took a different course: in jumping over, I heard and saw him jump into the water. I described Smith to the constable, and in eleven hours found him in custody. I found six pieces of lead moved under a wall rolled up, and the roll that they threw off the water closet; most all of it was taken off the water closet; there was about one hundred weight and a half. I have since fitted it, and it tallies. I believe Smith to be the other man, but cannot swear to him. I saw Sack taken, he had a blue frock on.
GEORGE TAYLOR. I am a constable of Clerkenwell. I took charge of Sack. I afterwards took Smith. On the afternoon of the robbery, I saw three of them in company together; the prisoners were two of them. I saw them by Clerkenwell church, in the neighbourhood of the prosecutor's house. I afterwards, from the description Court gave me, took Smith at eleven o'clock at night in bed; he was going to his chest to take a pair of stockings out; I told him to put on the same that he wore in the day. I took them up and they were wet nearly as high as the calf of the legs. He put on a velveteen jacket; I had seen him in the day time with a white jean jacket, and saw that jacket in the bed he was laying on; his shoes were also wet. I compared them with the marks in the mud in the field where they jumped over; they fitted all the way along from garden to garden.
JAMES BARBER. The building is mine; there was one hundred weight and a half of lead stolen.
SACK'S Defence. I was returning from Battle Bridge, heard a call of murder; got on the rails and saw three men run away; the man came up and secured me.
SMITH'S Defence. I was at home in bed at the time.
JOSEPH AVERY. I work at putting down gas pipes, and have slept with Smith every night for two years; he did not have the shoes on which the officer took away: I was in bed when they came. The white jacket on the bed was mine; the shoes belonged to the prisoner, and he did not wear them that day, and I am quite sure they were quite clean, dry, and oiled; he wore them on Sunday, and I never saw them on his feet after - I am perfectly sure they were quite clean, dry, and oiled. We went to bed about 10 o'clock, and the officer came in about twenty minutes: I was asleep when they came in; the officers asked for me, they did not ask for Smith; they took his strong shoes first, and the others next morning.
SACK - GUILTY. Aged 17.
SMITH - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years
First Middlesex Jury, before Wm. Arabin, Esq.
[trialAccount t18210411-201]

687. SARAH PORTER was indicted for stealing, the 14th of March, one seal, value 15 s.; one key, value 1 s., the goods of Richard Brinsley Smith, from his person.
RICHARD BRINSLEY SMITH. I live at No. 11, Denzel-street, Clare-market, am a bookseller, and clerk to a solicitor. Last Monday week, about half past eleven o'clockat night, I was returning from the Bagnigge Wells tavern in company with three others; the prisoner met us between Bagnigge Wells and the Cobham's Head, near the turnpike, she was with two other women. We met them face to face, and were pushing past them to go on. We did not stop them. The prisoner made a snatch at my seals, the chain broke, and she got my seals and key; it was gold, and worth 15 s. I laid hold of her immediately, stood before her to ascertain that it was gone, and while I was feeling in my fob, she set off to her companions; I followed and seized her. I have not found them. The other women were not stopped. I was quite sober.
GEORGE TAYLOR. I am a constable. The prisoner was delivered to my charge; I searched her, but did not find the property. I did not see the other women.
Prisoner's Defence. I say it is false. I was talking to these young women, who I knew by sight. The two gentlemen came up and began talking to them. I walked away, he followed, and said I robbed him.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Judgment respited.
First Middlesex Jury, before the Common Sergeant.

688. ANN WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, the sum of 9 s. in monies numbered, the property of William Hill, from his person.
WILLIAM HILL. I am a watch-gilder, and live in Great Sutton-street, Clerkenwell. On the morning of the 8th of April, after two o'clock, I was returning from my master's house, in Duke's-street, Lincoln's-inn-fields. I had been to Trinity-square part of the way home with a friend. I had business with my master, which kept me late. I was quite sober. In passing through White-cross-street I met this woman; she wanted me to go home with her. I said I had a home of my own. I had got my hand in my right-hand pocket with my silver in my hand; I let go of it and took my hand out, in order to disengage myself from her, as she had hold of me; I did not perceive her hand in my pocket; but the instant she turned from me I missed my silver - there was 10 s. 6 d.; 1 s. and 6 d. were left; I lost 9 s. among which was a half crown. Nobody was near enough to do it but her. I took her to the watch-house, and gave her in charge, stating what money I had lost. She denied having it, and said she had no money. The officer found it on her - there was a half crown, 5 s. and three sixpences. She had no more money.
WILLIAM PARTRIDGE. I am constable of the night. The prisoner was brought in by Hill; he said he had lost 9 s., among which was a half crown piece. I asked her if she had the money? She said she had none whatever about her. I searched her, and in her right-hand pocket I found a half crown piece, 2 s. and a 6 d., and down her bosom I found 3 s. and two sixpences. She said next morning that the prosecutor gave her part of it.
GUILTY. Aged 30.
Transported for Seven Years.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

689. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of March, one spade, value 1 s., and one shovel, value 1 s., the goods of Robert Doe.
ROBERT DOE. I am a cow-keeper, and live in the City-road. The prisoner is a stranger to me. The spade and shovel were in my cow yard, near Goswell-street. The prisoner was brought to me by an officer. I never gave him authority to take them.
WILLIAM EDWARDS. I am a spring-maker. I was standing in Henry-street, Old-street-road, about a quarter of a mile from the cow-yard, and saw the prisoner and three more young lads coming from across some ruins in a direction from the cow-yard, they stopped against the corner of a stable which I knew had been robbed. I went over the way to see if they had any thing; they retreated when I went towards them. I got over the pailing after them, and the shovel and spade were thrown down by one of them, all four ran away. I overtook the prisoner, and asked how he came by them? he said, he had not stolen them, but the boys had, and he was going with them to White-cross-street to sell them. Doe's name was on them.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I met these boys as I was going to work, they said they would come with me; I turned aside for something, they followed me. The man said,
"What have you got?" they then threw them down, and ran away.
GUILTY. Aged 10.
Transported for Seven Years.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18210411-206]
690. MICHAEL NEAL was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of March, one coat, value 3 l., the goods of Henry Arthur Broughton.
MR. HONE conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT REYNOLDS. I am groom to Mr. Henry Arthur Broughton, of Great Marlborough-street, solicitor. On the 6th of March I was going with my master to Clarendon-place, Edgware-road, in a gig. On arriving there I got out of the gig and rang the bell; it was about half past six o'clock in the evening. When I returned from ringing the bell, I saw the wheel of a cart locked into the wheel of the buggy. It is a wide road. I had drawn up as close as possible to the foot way. There was room for four carriages to pass without touching us. There were at least six persons in the cart; it was full of men and one boy. Master's box coat was in the buggy over his knees; we had the head up, it being a wet night. I saw the prisoner in possession of the coat about ten minutes after I rang the bell. We were expostulating with them for coming in contact with the gig, and begging them to unlock the wheels. Master ordered me to pursue the prisoner; I overtook him about a hundred and twenty yards from the gig, and saw the coat on his arm; he dropped it, and put himself in a fighting posture. I drew back, and stooped to pick up the coat, and he gave me a very severe blow between the two eyes; my hat fell off. The blow knocked me down. Several of the party who had been in the cart were behind, and I received some kicks from them as I lay in the road. The prisoner picked up a silk handkerchief which flew out of my hat, and ran away with it; he came back in about a quarter of an hour, to enquire what was the matter, and was secured.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. - Q. This was about seven o'clock? - A. Yes; I could identify any man's face at the time. The coat was thrown across the road first, and put back in the gig by a gentleman's servant, and put in the gig while I held the horse. There might be thirty people about or more. I heard there had been a bull bait in the neighbourhood. The people in the cart came round and said, I hope you are not hurt; and they felt about my breeches and almost pulled my watch out. I got some severe blows; my nose is all on one side now with it.
MR. JAMES SADLER. I live next door to Mr. Broughton, in Clarendon-place. On Tuesday evening at 6 o'clock I was in Clarendon-place, and saw the cart locked in the wheel of Mr. Broughton's gig, and saw the prisoner going off with the box coat. I followed him with Reynolds as quick as possible, overtook him, and he dropped it. Reynolds being nearest received a blow as we were both stooping to pick it up.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. There was great confusion? - A. Four persons were running; I believe all four came out of the cart. I picked up the coat. One of the people in the cart took it, and it was returned. I don't know who took it the second time.
WILLIAM FISHER. I am a Bow-street patrol. I was on duty, and assisted in securing the prisoner.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined One Year, and twice publicly Whipped.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18210411-207]
691. JOHN MORGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of April, one tub, value 3 d., and 70 lbs. of butter, value 2 l. 16 s., the goods of Wm. Palmer.
The prosecutor did not appear. NOT GUILTY.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18210411-208]
692. EDWARD LONG was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of April, 24 lbs. of lead, value 2 s. 6 d., belonging to Thomas Septimus Jones, fixed to a dwelling-house of his.
SECOND COUNT, stating it to be fixed to a building of his.
THOMAS SEPTIMUS JONES. I am the owner of a house in Curtain-road. I saw it about a fortnight previous to the robbery, the lead-work was complete then. The house was untenanted. On the 6th of April I received information; went and found all the water pipe cut from the back kitchen.
JOSEPH READY. I am a hat-maker, and work next door to the house. On the 3d of April, about half past nine at night, I was alarmed by a noise in the next house; I gave the alarm to the person who had the letting of the house. I went into the house, and opened the door myself with the key, and in the top room I found the prisoner alone; I gave him to the watchman. After he was put in the watch-house, I went down into the kitchen, and found the lead laying on the floor; it was torn from the wall; there was about nine feet of pipe, fresh torn, and cut in different pieces.
WILLIAM MORETON. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house; next morning I went to the kitchen and found the pipe torn off the wall; it weighed 24 lbs. I found him in great distress.
Prisoner's Defence. The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Confined Three Months.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18210411-209]
692. MARY HARTNET was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of April, five yards of cotton, value 5 s. and one petticoat, value 4 s. the goods of John Brooks, privately in his shop.
JOHN BROOKS. I live at 54, High-street, St. Giles's. I am a silversmith and pawnbroker; these things were forfeited pledges, and were in the shop for sale, and hung by the side of the wall about three feet from the door, inside the shop; I had seen the petticoat safe two hours before, and the cotton I saw the first thing in the morning. About 3 o'clock in the afternoon the prisoner came and produced an apron to pawn for 1 s. I refused to take it, as it was not worth that; she left, and in ten minutes I missed the petticoat, and in about a quarter of an hour I was fetched to Mr. Hulme's, the pawnbroker in Museum-street, and found the prisoner there in custody, and 5 yards of printed cotton on the counter; it had the sale ticket on it in my writing, that would not be seen till it was unfolded; it was mine. I then asked what she had got in her apron, she said what was that to me. I opened it, and there was the petticoat; my shop ticket belonging to that was on the floor torn in pieces close to her feet; she was secured. My apprentice had been serving in the shop; he could give me no information about it; he stood about the middle of the shop while the prisoner was there. I was the first person that spoke to her - she was two or three minutes in the shop, and had no conversation with the apprentice; he was ticketing goods. When I gave her her answer I believe I went out to the parlour. The cotton cost me 5 s. and the petticoat 4 s.
THOMAS PERRY. I am apprentice to Mr. Hulme, pawnbroker, No. 53, Museum-street, Bloomsbury. On the 14th of April, the prisoner and another woman came into the shop about 3 o'clock, and offered an apron in pawn for 1 s. the prisoner presented it, I advanced 9 d. on it: they went away, and in a few minutes the prisoner returned alone, and took out of her apron a petticoat, which she put on the counter, and then seeing the shop ticket on it, drew it back; I saw her fumbling down with it, which gave me suspicion. She immediately took the cotton out of her apron and offered that; when she put it on the counter, she saw the ticket on that Mr. Hulme laid hold of it, and she pulled to get it from him, he got it from her and kept it. We examined the ticket and saw it was Brooks's, and sent to him: he claimed it and the petticoat. I have had it ever since.
JOHN BROOKS. The ticket is mine and was in the cotton.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN TAYLOR. I am one of the beadles of St. George's, Bloomsbury. I was sent for, and took charge of her; I found 15 duplicates on her - none of them have been claimed.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to pawn an apron; the otherwoman pushed something off the guard and gave it me; when I came out I went to pawn them.
GUILTY. Aged 52.
Transported for life.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18210411-210]
694. JAMES HIPP was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of March, 17 s. 6 d. the property of William Mansfield.
WILLIAM MANSFIELD. I live in Union-street, Shadwell. The prisoner was a stranger to me. On Shrove Tuesday I was employed by Mr. Elliot, a wine merchant, to take some wine. I sent the prisoner to Poplar with some goods. He was to have 17 s. back. He brought it and delivered it to my wife. I never saw the money. My wife is not here.
NOT GUILTY.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18210411-211]
695. JOSEPH BOLTON was indicted for stealing on the 6th of June, 1819, twelve pounds of lead, value 2 s. and ten pounds of copper, value 12 s. the goods of Thomas Richardson, (since deceased), and fixed to a building of his.
JAMES SALTER. I am a gardener, at Fulham. I knew Mr. Thomas Richardson when he was alive. This lead was on the outside of the privy, - the copper joined it, and covered an other building. I was called up about twelve in the night of the 5th of June, 1819, to go to market, and I saw two persons drop from Mr. Richardson's back wall. I went after James Dormas whom I took, - he was tried and convicted. The other I did not know. The prisoner lived in the neighbourhood about a fortnight after the robbery, and then left.
SARAH ROWLAND. I was servant to Mr. Richardson, he has been dead two years on the 5th of July; he was alive at the time of the robbery, his name was Thomas. Some part of the privy was robbed before. After Dormas was taken, I found it completely stripped.
JAMES SHARP. I drive Salter's horse. I was up on that morning, and saw the two men come over the wall. I cannot say the prisoner was one.
R. ELLINGHAM. I apprehended the prisoner, and we told him the charges. He said he was willing to go with me.
NOT GUILTY.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18210411-212]
696. JOHN WELLS was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of March, one saddle, value 2 s.; one halter, value 6 d.; and one bridle value 2 s.; the goods of Samuel Dalton, Barnaby Sanders, and William Wood.
SAMUEL DALTON. I am a cornchandler. Barnaby Sanders and William Wood are joint executors with me to the estate of Eliza Dalton. The property was in a stable in Pratt's Mews, Camden-town.
WILLIAM HALES. I keep a horse and cart. About five or six weeks ago the prisoner worked for me, - we were out selling sand, - he asked if I wanted to buy a saddle and halter, as he knew a person who had one to sell. I said, I do not want them, - he afterwards said they were very good, - I said I would look at them on Sunday. I saw them in the stable. I do not know who put them there. When I saw him, I said I was informed he had been and got 4 s. from my wife. He now denied selling them to my wife. I told him I should have nothing to do with them, and to bring the young man, or I would kick him and the harness out together. He absconded.
Cross-examined by MR. HONE. Q. What trade are you? - A. I carry out sand and bricks.
ELIZA HALES. I am the wife of the last witness. I never saw the harness. The prisoner brought me some money his master had sent him for, and he said,
"Mistress you are to give me 4 s. 6 d.; I am going to buy a saddle and halter for 4 s." I did not like to give him the money, but I did.
REBECCA PRICE. I was with Mrs. Hales, and heard the prisoner ask for 4 s. to buy a saddle and halter; she gave it to him at last. I never saw the things.
JOHN DAVIS. I am a Bow-street patrole. I apprehended the prisoner last Friday. I told him what I took him for, he said he was not the only one to blame, and said Hales sent him for it.
NOT GUILTY.
First Middlesex Jury before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18210411-213]

The Prosecutor did not appear. NOT GUILTY.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
